I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I mean, I’m still trying to do things, so it’s not like I’ve completely stopped living or anything. But I used to really make time for hiking, yoga, my garden, taking bird photos… those were kind of my things. Lately I keep canceling plans or telling myself I’ll do it later, and my camera’s just been sitting in the closet. It all feels like more effort than it’s worth, which… I don’t know, maybe that sounds silly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t want to alarm you, because I haven’t made any kind of plan or anything like that. But… yeah, sometimes late at night I catch myself thinking that maybe my husband would be better off without me.\n\nI know that sounds awful. I’m sorry. It’s not like I want to do something exactly, but the thought keeps coming back enough that it scares me a little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had another period like this earlier in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean… I’ve had rough patches before, like anyone, but I don’t know if they were like this or if I just pushed through them and didn’t really label it. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energetic or wired, much more than your usual self?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, maybe. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because at the time I told myself I was just being productive. But there was this burst where I really couldn’t sit still. I kept jumping up to sketch out office layouts, reorganize files, start one more task… even when there wasn’t any real reason it had to be done right then.\n\nDoes that count as what you mean by wired?\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you need much less sleep than usual and still feel rested?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure, honestly. I don’t remember it as clearly as, like, “I slept three hours and felt amazing” or anything. I may have stayed up later because I was busy doing things, but I don’t know that I needed much less sleep and felt rested, if that makes sense.\n\nSorry, I know that’s probably not the cleanest answer.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually confident or like you could do things better than other people?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I feel kind of embarrassed saying it that way. At the time it felt more like ambition than being overly confident.\n\nI remember thinking I was going to outgrow my current firm really quickly, and that I could start my own consulting business and probably do it better than the people around me. Which… sounds arrogant now, especially because I hadn’t actually lined up clients or anything concrete yet. But in my head then, it felt very doable. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, were you getting much more done than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it looked like productivity, which is why I didn’t really question it at first.\n\nI was making all these new research templates, drafting pitch ideas, and even contacting potential clients after hours, like I was going to launch the business right away. And the “office” was still basically an unfinished outbuilding, so… looking back, it was probably a lot more than my usual pace.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of “getting more done” you mean, but yes, I was definitely doing more.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you spend much more money than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big obvious way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I may have bought some things related to the business idea, or spent a little more on stuff that felt “necessary” at the time, but I don’t remember anything like huge shopping sprees or draining savings. So I’d probably say no, or at least not that I noticed. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did it cause serious problems at work or at home?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it completely blew things up or anything… but yes, it did start causing problems.\n\nAt work, I fell behind on some client reports because I was putting so much energy into the business idea and all the extra planning. And at home, I kind of let the steadier things slip — seeing friends, getting outside, doing the routines that usually keep me grounded.\n\nI guess now most of my energy goes into just getting through work and keeping up with the basics at home. I don’t know if that counts as “serious,” but it has definitely made things harder.\n\nDoctor: About that energized period, roughly how many days did it last?\n\nPatient: I’m a little fuzzy on the exact number of days, honestly. Since we took on the mortgage about two months ago, it’s felt like my mood swung from that overcharged business push into the low stretch I’m in now.\n\nSo… the energized part wasn’t just an afternoon or one good day. It was somewhere in that earlier part of the last couple months, but I’m not sure I can count the days neatly. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, were you talking much more than usual or feeling pressure to keep talking?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t remember people telling me I was talking nonstop or anything like that.\n\nI may have been more enthusiastic when I was explaining the idea, maybe a little hard to interrupt because I was excited, but I don’t know that I felt pressured to keep talking. So I’d probably say not really, unless there’s a version of that I’m not recognizing.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did your thoughts feel like they were racing?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t remember it feeling like my thoughts were racing in a dramatic way, like I couldn’t control them.\n\nIt was more that I had a lot on my mind and kept going back to the same plans and ideas. Maybe that’s sort of what you mean? But I don’t want to overstate it — I’m not sure I’d call it “racing thoughts.”\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel driven to start lots of projects or stay unusually busy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that part does fit, probably. I don’t want to make it sound too grand, but I was doing a lot.\n\nI started making research templates, drafting pitch ideas, and reaching out to potential clients after hours, like the business was basically ready to launch right then. And really, the “office” was still just an unfinished outbuilding, so… looking back, it may have been more intense than I was admitting at the time. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say “euphoric” exactly… that sounds a little extreme. But in the first few weeks after we signed the mortgage papers, I did feel kind of lit up, like everything was possible and I could just make it all happen.\n\nThen, honestly, I’d swing into getting irritated really fast — like snapping at my husband or coworkers over small delays. I’m not proud of that. So yes, there was definitely an unusually good, unstoppable feeling at times, but it wasn’t just happy the whole time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did that unusually energized or irritable period last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… not every minute of every day, but since we took on the mortgage about two months ago, it’s felt like I went from that really overcharged business push into this low stretch I’m in now.\n\nSo the energized or irritable part was more than just a day or two. I guess it did last at least four days in a row, if I’m understanding the question right.\n\nDoctor: During that energized or irritable period, were you hospitalized for it?\n\nPatient: No, I wasn’t hospitalized for it. It got intense, but not to the point where anyone sent me to the hospital or anything like that. I just… tried to keep functioning, I guess."
